      - A unit added that way stays only while a brand row is in it: picking another unit, or removing the row, takes it back out (`DropUnusedAddedUnits`), so trying units out leaves nothing behind. Save rejects one whose quantity is still empty.
      - The grid's add row edits the screen's own row (`NewBrandPriceRow`, through `controls:GridAddNewRow.ItemFactory`), which talks to the screen through `IBrandPriceRowHost`: a unit picked there is added before the row is committed, and its buffered price follows the buffer. (The single-price picker it replaced had to keep a `ComboBoxAdv`'s items in place while it handled a pick, or it fell back to its first item. The grid's unit choices don't change when a unit is picked, since they already list every unit of measure.)
    - The cards follow the form, not the selection. Purchase Cost Setup follows the editing source, the BOM card `IsEditingMade` and the Linked Cost card `IsEditingLinked`, so a new material shows every card before its first Save. Price History (`ShowPriceHistory`) only shows once a purchased material is saved.
  - **What Save sends** to `IRawMaterialRepository.SaveAsync(material, units, brandPrices, bomLines)`:
    - An edit still open in the Alternative Units grid is committed when focus leaves it (`controls:GridEditCommit.OnFocusLeave`), so clicking Save includes it.
    - `units` is the complete list.
    - For a purchased material, every brand row (a row left entirely blank is skipped), each naming its unit by unit of measure, since a unit added in the same save has no id yet. A row missing its unit or price, in a unit the form doesn't have, or repeating a brand in the same unit is refused first. `null` for any other material, which leaves brand prices alone.
    - A made ingredient's add-ins only when they differ from the ones last loaded (BOM lines, the same way, until 2026-10-27). Untouched add-ins aren't replaced.
    - Afterwards `LoadCommand` runs, then a fresh copy of the material is selected, so the form reloads what was saved: units with ids, the brand prices, the price history and the BOM.
  - **How it saves: one context and one transaction.**
    - Every step goes through the `internal static` write methods the single-purpose repository methods now share: `RawMaterialRepository.AddAsync`/`UpdateAsync`, `RawMaterialUnitRepository.AddAsync`/`UpdateAsync`/`DeleteAsync`, `RawMaterialBrandPriceRepository.RemoveAllAsync`/`AddAllAsync`/`RepriceAsync` and `IngredientBomLineRepository.SetLinesAsync`. Each takes the caller's `TinapayanRMSDbContext`, saves to it, and says whether the cost cascade must run.
    - Order: the material; the old brand prices go (so a unit only they were in can be removed); its units, removed first, then updated, then added (so a replacement fits within the limit of two, and a removed unit of measure can come back as a new row); the new brand prices, then the reprice, compared with the cost from before the save so at most one price is logged; the BOM. Each step saves as it goes, so the next step's checks see what it wrote.
    - A rejection anywhere rolls all of it back, and the cascade runs once, after the commit.
    - The caller's entities are only read, so a rolled-back save never leaves an id on them.
    - `SetLinesAsync` gained the transaction its delete-then-insert lacked.
    - The public single-purpose methods keep their behaviour, and their tests keep covering the checks; `RawMaterialSavePersistenceTests` covers the whole save.
  - **Refreshes keep unsaved units.** `LoadCommand` points each row at its refreshed unit of measure (so a rename in Settings shows) instead of rebuilding the rows from the refreshed material.
    - `NewCommand` clears the details itself: after an unsaved new material nothing was selected, so the selection subscriptions don't fire.
    - Delete Price stays immediate and reloads only the price history, where selecting the refreshed material would drop unsaved edits. It changes no cost, so nothing else reloads.
- **One Products screen (`ProductsView`/`ProductsViewModel`)**, rebuilt from the user's Cream Loaf / Chocolate Cake mockups (2026-09-14): the Raw Materials shape, with one Save. Its design notes, every feature it gained and the reasons behind them, are in **[docs/plan/products-screen.md](docs/plan/products-screen.md)**.
- **The Settings dialog holds the lookups every screen picks from: units of measure, categories, and (since 2026-09-14) sub-assembly roles** (2026-09-13, at the user's request: the embedded Manage categories panels were hard to find, and Units of Measure took a whole tab). Since 2026-09-17 it has two category tabs: **Material Categories** (the raw materials' list, `CategoriesView`, formerly just "Categories") and **Product Categories** (`ProductCategoriesView`/`ProductCategoriesViewModel`, the same shape over `IProductCategoryRepository`).
  - **It opens from the gear at the bottom of the navigation drawer.** Until 2026-09-22 that was a `FooterItems` entry with `ItemType="Button"` — Syncfusion's docs say a Button-type item "is not selectable and only ItemClicked and Command actions can be performed", so opening Settings left the current page highlighted, and Syncfusion's own `DisplayMode.xaml` demo puts Settings in `FooterItems` too. With the drawer now in `Default` mode, `FooterItems` isn't rendered at all: Settings is a `ButtonAdv` pinned below the menu's `ListBox`, **outside** it, which is what keeps it from taking the menu's selection — the same guarantee by a different route. Verified in the app: opening Settings closes the menu and leaves the highlight on the page showing.
  - **`SettingsWindow`** is a modal `Window` owned by `MainWindow`, with fixed `TabControlExt` tabs (like Products' sub-tabs: no close buttons or context menus) for `UnitsOfMeasureView`, `CategoriesView` and `SubAssemblyRolesView` (and `ProductCategoriesView` and `CostingDefaultsView` since), and a Close button (`IsCancel`, so Esc closes it too). It takes the main window's theme (`SfSkinManager.SetTheme(settings, SfSkinManager.GetTheme(this))`) and sets `SizeMode="Default"` itself (`Touch` until 2026-09-15), since a new window is a new visual tree.
  - It's **built afresh each time it opens, over singleton ViewModels** (`UnitsOfMeasureViewModel`, `CategoriesViewModel`), so a tab keeps its selection and unsaved edits between visits. Anything a view hooks onto its longer-lived ViewModel is undone with it: `SettingsWindow` registers the delete confirmations and disposes them when it closes (`RegisterNativeMessageBoxHandler` returns the registration for this), and each view's focus-the-Name-box subscription lives from `Loaded` to `Unloaded`.
    - **Its controls' own ties to what outlives it are undone on `Closed` too (`ReleaseControls`, 2026-09-25)**. Before, every closed Settings stayed in memory for the rest of the session, and its grids went on taking in each reload of the ViewModels' lists. Found while checking the app against Infragistics' "15 WPF Performance Tips"; the other tips were already met or don't apply. Three Syncfusion behaviours, each confirmed by decompiling 34.2.9 and by heap dumps (`dotnet-dump` `gcroot`):
      - An `SfDataGrid`'s view subscribes strongly to its `ItemsSource`'s `CollectionChanged` and holds the grid. Only `Dispose` unhooks it; `OnUnloaded` doesn't.
      - An editable `ComboBoxAdv` in `Suggest` mode (every picker, via `App.ApplyPickerDefaults`) combines its filter into the list's shared default view, and takes it off only while it's loaded.
      - An `SfTextBoxExt` that loads before its template has found its window hooks `Application.Current.Windows[0]`'s `MouseDown` (the main window), then unhooks from its own window instead, so only its `Dispose` removes it. In Settings that's the Name box of every tab but the first. `TabControlExt` loads every tab's content when the window opens, and a tab not showing isn't templated until it's first shown, so this happened on every open, whether or not those tabs were visited.
      - So the window disposes each grid and text box, and takes each picker's filter back off its view, walking the logical tree to reach the tabs not showing. A grid's `SelectedItem` binding and a text box's bindings are cleared first: `SfTextBoxExt.Dispose` sets `Text` to empty, which would reach the ViewModel through the two-way binding. A tab still keeps its selection and unsaved edits between visits.
      - Measured off-screen against the dev database, with a probe that opens and closes Settings visiting every tab under a themed stand-in main window: 5 of 5 closed dialogs survived a full GC before, 0 of 5 after. The new-category prompt (`TextPromptWindow`) never leaked. Any new window built per use over long-lived ViewModels needs the same care.
  - Each opening loads both lists. When it closes, `MainWindow` refreshes the screen on show so its pickers include anything added or renamed; the other tabs pick changes up when they're next shown, as for any change.
  - **Both tabs reset their form after Save and Delete**, as New does, and title it "New Unit"/"New Category" or "Edit Unit"/"Edit Category". Before, Save and Delete cleared the list selection but left the form pointing at the last record, so the next Save silently renamed it (or failed, after a Delete) instead of adding one. The user ran into this while asking how to add a category.
- **Migrations that carry data over use hand-added SQL, each verified up and down on a restored copy of the dev database** — the three multi-unit migrations and the raw materials merge (`BACKUP … COPY_ONLY` / `RESTORE … WITH MOVE`, then `dotnet ef database update --connection` on the copy):
  - `IngredientAlternativeUnits`: each purchase unit + `PurchaseUnitQty` becomes an `IngredientUnit`, prices are linked to it, `CostPerGram` is recomputed from the true latest price (healing the same-day bug), then the old columns are dropped. The scaffold dropped the columns first; the operations were reordered so the SQL runs in between.
  - `RecipeLinesInAlternativeUnits`: `Quantity = Grams` for existing lines.
  - `PackagingMaterialAlternativeUnits`: rewritten by hand — the scaffold took `PurchaseUnitQty → CostPerBaseUnit` for a rename (both decimals). A purchase unit other than the base unit becomes a `PackagingMaterialUnit` with prices linked to it; a purchase unit equal to the base unit turns prices into per-base-unit prices; `CostPerBaseUnit` comes from the latest price. `Down` restates everything in the old one-purchase-unit shape, including packaging lines entered in an alternative unit.
  - `MergeRawMaterials` (the raw materials merge): rewritten by hand — the scaffold dropped the eight old tables before creating the four new ones. It creates `RawMaterialCategories`, `RawMaterials`, `RawMaterialUnits` and `RawMaterialPriceHistories` first, copies everything across in one SQL batch (table variables map old packaging ids to new ones), then drops the old tables. Ingredient rows keep their ids (`IDENTITY_INSERT`), so recipe lines, BOM lines and their units still point at them; packaging materials, units and prices get new ids after them, and packaging lines follow. A packaging category joins the ingredient category of the same name (case-insensitively, like the unique index), and a packaging material named like an ingredient gets " (packaging)" appended, since names are now unique across both. `Down` splits the tables back by type with every id kept, each category going to both old category tables. Verified on a restored copy of the dev database — the user's 4 ingredients, 5 categories, 5 units and 5 prices came through with identical ids and costs — then with packaging data seeded into the old schema (a category differing only in case, a material named like an ingredient, units, prices per unit and per base unit, and packaging lines in both): up, down and up again. The dev database was backed up (`COPY_ONLY`) before the migration was applied to it.
  - `AddCostBuffer`: adds `CostBufferPercent` and `BufferedCostPerBaseUnit` (both default 0), sets `BufferedCostPerBaseUnit = CostPerBaseUnit` for every material (no buffer yet, so no cost changes), then adds `CK_RawMaterials_CostBufferPercent`. Verified up, down and up on a restored copy (every row's buffered cost matched its cost; the constraint rejected 101%), then applied to the dev database after a `COPY_ONLY` backup.
  - `ProductDescriptionUpdatedAtAndAdministrativeCost` (2026-09-14) needed no hand SQL: it only adds columns, `Description` (`nvarchar(500)`) and `UpdatedAt` (`datetime2`), both nullable, on Products, and `AdministrativeCost` (`decimal(18,4) NOT NULL DEFAULT 0`) on CostingParameters. Applied to the dev database after a `COPY_ONLY` backup.
  - `ProductCategories` (2026-09-17) needed no hand SQL: it adds the `ProductCategories` table (unique `Name`) and a nullable `Products.CategoryId` with a Set Null FK, so existing products stay uncategorized. Applied to the dev database.
  - `ProductCurrentPrice` (2026-09-19) needed no hand SQL: it only adds a nullable `Products.CurrentPrice` (`decimal(18,4)`), so existing products have none entered. Applied to the dev database.
  - `ProductOwnedSubAssemblies` (2026-09-14): rewritten by hand — the scaffold dropped `ProductTabLinks` outright and added `OwnerProductId` as 0. In order:
    1. **Role-tab links become sub-assembly links.** A recursive CTE follows each role-tab link (`TabKind = 0`) to the end of its chain; the linking product's own, hidden usages in that role are deleted, and the end product's usages in that role are copied to it at the same weights. Then the role-tab rows go.
    2. **Sub-assemblies no product uses are deleted** (with their lines): they can't have an owner.
    3. **Owners**: `OwnerProductId` is added nullable, set to the lowest product id using each sub-assembly, then made NOT NULL with its Cascade FK; the unique `Name` index becomes `(OwnerProductId, Name)`.
    4. **`ProductTabLinks` → `PackagingLinks`** (`RenameTable`, keeping the Packaging-tab rows): `TabKind`, `SubAssemblyRoleId`, their FK and the filtered indexes go; the PK and FKs are re-created under the new name, with a plain unique index on `ProductId`.
    5. **Grams-only lines**: `Quantity` and `UnitId` (FK, index) leave `SubAssemblyLines` and `IngredientBomLines`; `Grams` already held every line's amount.
    6. **Piece size**: `WeightPerPiece` is renamed `AuthoredValue` and `AuthoredField` added at 0 (weight per piece), so every usage keeps its weight; then `CK_ProductSubAssemblyUsages_AuthoredValue`, and later `ShrinkagePercent` at 0 with `CK_ProductSubAssemblyUsages_ShrinkagePercent` (`UsageShrinkagePercent`), so every existing usage keeps its yield.
    - `Down` resolves a typed yield back to a weight (`TotalWeight / yield`), gives lines `Quantity = Grams` with no unit, restores `ProductTabLinks` with every row a Packaging tab, and appends " (owner name)" to a sub-assembly name that another owner also uses before restoring the global unique index. It can't restore role-tab links (they stay the shared usages they became) or the units lines were entered in; the migration's comment says so.
    - Verified up, down and up on a restored copy of `TinapayanRMS_Demo`: every product's sub-assembly cost per piece matched before and after each step (22.567141, 10.322941, 14.028705); Cream Loaf came to own Sweet Dough, Butter Cream, Custard Filling and Crumb Topping, and Cream Loaf Mini's Dough-tab link became a link to Sweet Dough at 60 g; the two lines entered in units kept their grams. Then, seeded into the new schema, a typed yield (100 pieces → 1 g a piece down), a sub-assembly name shared by two owners (renamed down) and a packaging link (kept both ways). Applied to the dev database (no products or sub-assemblies yet; its one BOM line kept its 500 g) after a `COPY_ONLY` backup.
  - `CostingPercentagesOfMaterialCost` (2026-09-15) needed no hand SQL: EF scaffolded three `RenameColumn`s on CostingParameters (`LaborCost` → `LaborPercent`, `OverheadCost` → `OverheadPercent`, `AdministrativeCost` → `AdministrativePercent`), same `decimal(18,4)`. **The numbers carry over unchanged, so a saved ₱4 labor cost reads as 4%.** Converting them would mean working out every product's material cost in SQL (sub-assembly piece sizes, buffered costs, packaging through its link chain), and the dev database had no costing rows to convert; only `TinapayanRMS_Demo`'s three products have old peso amounts. Verified up, down and up on a restored copy of `TinapayanRMS_Demo` (the values survived each step), then applied to the dev database after a `COPY_ONLY` backup. In the app on that copy: Cream Loaf's ₱23.2754 material cost at 25%, 10% and 5% came to ₱5.8189, ₱2.3275 and ₱1.1638, a ₱32.5856 subtotal and ₱47.44 with a 30% markup and 12% VAT; Save stored 25, 10 and 5, and Reports showed the same ₱32.59 and ₱47.44.
  - `RawMaterialCostPrecision` (2026-09-19) needed no hand SQL: two `AlterColumn`s take `RawMaterials.CostPerBaseUnit` and `BufferedCostPerBaseUnit` from `decimal(18,4)` to `decimal(28,20)` (`HasPrecision` in `RawMaterialConfiguration`), so a buffered cost typed per bag or kilo is kept as typed once divided down to the base unit. EF warns of possible data loss because 8 integer digits replace 14; no cost per base unit comes near ₱100,000,000. 28 digits in all is what a .NET `decimal` holds, so any stored value reads back without overflowing (a larger scale, such as `decimal(38,28)`, would overflow on reading anything from ₱8 up). Existing values carry over unchanged. Applied to the dev database.
  - `StandaloneSubAssemblies` (2026-09-15), scaffolded by `dotnet ef migrations add` and left as it came (the user's instruction: never hand-write a migration): `OwnerProductId` becomes nullable (EF drops and re-creates `IX_SubAssemblies_OwnerProductId_Name` around it, unfiltered). **Its scaffolded `Down` can't run once a standalone sub-assembly exists**: it sets the missing owners to 0, which no product has, so the FK rejects it. Verified up, down and up on a restored copy of `TinapayanRMS_Demo` (no standalone rows yet; the column came back nullable and the index unfiltered), then applied to the dev database after a `COPY_ONLY` backup.
  - `SubAssembliesAllStandalone` (2026-09-23), scaffolded by `dotnet ef migrations add` and left as it came: drops the owner FK, `IX_SubAssemblies_OwnerProductId_Name` and `OwnerProductId`, makes `Name` NOT NULL, and adds a unique `IX_SubAssemblies_Name`. It needs every owned row converted first (the one-off script under SubAssembly's Existing data): an unconverted one would come out nameless, and the unique index would refuse the migration, not lose it quietly.
  - `ProductOwnedRoles` (2026-09-23), scaffolded and left as it came: `SubAssemblyRoles.IsProductOwned` (bit, default 0, with `UpdateData` rows setting the seeded roles' to false), and `SubAssemblies.OwnerProductId` (nullable) with its index and Cascade FK to `Products`. No data step: what the flag does to existing sub-assemblies is `SubAssemblyRoleRepository.UpdateAsync`'s, done by ticking the roles in the app.
  - Always run `dotnet ef migrations has-pending-model-changes` after adding a migration, and rebuild Infrastructure before `database update --no-build` — a build from before `migrations add` doesn't contain the new migration, and EF then reports pending model changes.
- **Off-screen renders verify the card layouts without touching the user's monitor:** a file-based app (`#:project` the UI csproj) creates `new App()` + `InitializeComponent()` for the resources, builds the real ViewModel against a database (read-only), selects an item, and renders through a `VisualBrush` so the scrolled detail column comes out in full. Match `MainWindow`'s size mode: `Default` since 2026-09-15, which a new window already has (before that, `SfSkinManager.SetSizeMode(window, SizeMode.Touch)`, or inputs rendered at the compact height). For demo data, render against a restored copy, never the dev database.
- **After a command changes a row's server-computed fields (Phase 5: `DeletePriceCommand` changing `Ingredient.CurrentCost`/`CostPerGram`), refresh that one row rather than reloading the whole screen**: re-fetch it via the repository's `GetByIdAsync`, `_cache.AddOrUpdate(refreshed)` to update the grid, and reassign the `Selected*` property to the refreshed instance — `SfDataGrid` tracks selection by reference, so leaving `Selected*` pointing at the stale object silently desyncs the form/detail panel from what the grid now shows.
- **`UpdateAsync` must apply fields one by one onto a tracked, freshly-loaded entity — never `dbContext.Set<T>().Update(incoming)` on an object the caller built from scratch.** `Update()` marks *every* property Modified, so an `Ingredient`/`PackagingMaterial` built by a Save command's form fields (which never sets `CurrentCost`/`CostPerGram`, since those are server-owned) would silently overwrite them back to null/0 on every plain rename — a real bug found in Phase 6 while wiring `WeightLossPercent` through the same path, present in both `IngredientRepository` and `PackagingMaterialRepository` since Phase 4 and fixed in both at once. The fix is the same shape every time: load the tracked entity by id, copy across only the user-editable fields, leave every server-computed field untouched, then `SaveChangesAsync`. A related, easy-to-miss corollary: if a user-editable field feeds a server-computed one, the `UpdateAsync` that edits it must recompute that derived field too — it doesn't get another chance to until the next price entry. Originally `PurchaseUnitQty` feeding `CostPerGram`; since the multi-unit work it's an alternative unit's multiplier, which `RawMaterialUnitRepository.UpdateAsync` applies to the cost (and, for ingredients, to recipe-line grams).
- **`ExecuteDeleteAsync` doesn't wrap a failure in `DbUpdateException` the way `SaveChangesAsync` does** — it issues a raw `DELETE` outside the change tracker, so a foreign-key violation surfaces as the driver's own exception (`Npgsql.PostgresException` since 2026-10-21; `SqlException` on SQL Server before) instead. A test asserting `ThrowAsync<DbUpdateException>()` around an `ExecuteDeleteAsync` call needs `PostgresException` instead (found in Phase 6, verifying `IngredientBomLine`'s Restrict-delete FK).
- **FluentValidation (Phase 13) is a thin, UI-facing pre-check layer added *alongside*, never in place of, the existing `ApplicationCore` Rules classes.** One `AbstractValidator<T>` per entity that actually has an editable Save form mirrors the same rules the repository already enforces via `if/throw`. There are ten: `RawMaterial`, `RawMaterialUnit`, `RawMaterialBrandPrice`, `RawMaterialCategory`, `ProductCategory`, `UnitOfMeasure`, `SubAssemblyRole`, `SubAssembly`, `Product` and `CostingParameters`, which also checks the Settings dialog's costing defaults. The Phase 13 set was `Ingredient`, `IngredientUnit`, `PackagingMaterial`, `PackagingMaterialUnit`, `SubAssemblyRole`, `SubAssembly`, `Product`, `CostingParameters`, `IngredientPriceHistory` and `PackagingPriceHistory`, and the lookups, read-only then, had none. Categories and units of measure got theirs when they became editable (2026-09-12), the raw materials merge folded the ingredient and packaging validators together (2026-09-13), product categories arrived with one (2026-09-17), and brand prices replaced the price-history one (2026-09-18). A Save/Add command runs the validator first and, on failure, sets `ErrorMessage` and returns *before* calling the repository — the repository's own checks are untouched and remain the authoritative last line of defense, so nothing about the already-tested defensive checks changes; FluentValidation only turns an invalid Save into an immediate, friendly on-screen message instead of a thrown exception the user never sees the text of. A "replace-all" set of lines or usages (the usages and packaging lines the Products Save sends, and the BOM lines the Raw Materials Save sends) gets no validator of its own — that cross-row validation already lives in the `ApplicationCore` Rules class the repository calls.
- **`ViewModelBase` (Phase 13)** — a shared `ReactiveObject` base class, introduced only once four CRUD ViewModels needed identical behavior (not before): `ErrorMessage` (shown now by the error toast) and a ReactiveUI `Interaction<string, bool> ConfirmDelete` the View fulfills with a real dialog. Since then it has also gained `SuccessMessage` (the success toast), `IsRefreshing`/`ApplyRefresh` (a tab's view state, above) and `IsRestoring`/`ApplyRestore` (undo), with `IsBusyApplying` for either. **Superseded (2026-09-14):** `HasError` (an `ObservableAsPropertyHelper` derived from `ErrorMessage`) and `IsBusy` were removed when the error toast replaced the status banner and its busy bar (see the `ErrorToast` notes). Two protected helpers wrap every command that touches the database: `RunSafelyAsync(Func<Task> action)` clears `ErrorMessage` and `SuccessMessage` (closing their toasts), then maps three exception types to a friendly `ErrorMessage` (`InvalidOperationException` → `ex.Message` verbatim, since every Rules class already writes a clear message; `DbUpdateException` → `"That name is already in use."`, the only scenario that type is ever thrown for anywhere in this app; `SqlException` → `"This item is currently in use elsewhere and can't be deleted."`, matching the `ExecuteDeleteAsync`-throws-`SqlException` behavior documented above), logging each as a warning, and lets any other exception type propagate uncaught, since that would be a real bug, not a user-facing condition to mask; `RunWithConfirmationAsync(message, action)` shows the confirmation dialog first and only calls `RunSafelyAsync` if the user agrees. Every command that calls a repository Delete method is wrapped in `RunWithConfirmationAsync`; every Add/Update-calling command is wrapped in `RunSafelyAsync` (with a validator pre-check, per the FluentValidation point above, when one exists for that entity); a Set-lines/Set-usages command is `RunSafelyAsync` only; a command that only mutates an in-memory edit list (`Add*Line`/`Remove*Line`/`Add*Usage`/`Remove*Usage`) or `NewCommand` is left unwrapped, since it's reversible pre-Save and carries no exception risk. `ProductsViewModel`'s `CloneCommand`/`CreateVariantCommand` are `RunSafelyAsync` only, not confirmation-gated, since neither destroys data. Its Phase 10 tab-link commands (`LinkTabCommand`/`UnlinkTabCommand`) went with role-tab links on 2026-09-14: linking, unlinking and copying a sub-assembly are now recipe edits that Save writes.
- **Serilog (Phase 13)** writes a daily rolling file to `logs/tinapayanrms-.log` under the app's base directory, 31-day retention, configured once via a static `Log.Logger` built in `App`'s static constructor (so even a licensing failure in that same constructor gets logged) and registered in DI as a `Serilog.ILogger` singleton. Each `ViewModelBase`-derived class calls `logger.ForContext(GetType())` once, in the shared base constructor, to get a per-class-named logger without needing a separate DI registration per ViewModel. `App.OnStartup` also hooks `DispatcherUnhandledException`/`AppDomain.UnhandledException` to log any exception that reaches those handlers. **Since 2026-10-03 the app carries on after an unhandled exception on the UI thread** instead of closing: `OnDispatcherUnhandledException` logs it, shows its `Failure` text in a message box saying the app is still running and nothing unsaved was lost, and marks it handled. `UnhandledErrorGuard` lets the app close as before when more than 3 arrive within 10 s, since one thrown on every layout pass would otherwise be a message after a message. A failure ReactiveUI was left holding arrives wrapped in its `UnhandledErrorException`, and the cause inside is what's shown. `TaskScheduler.UnobservedTaskException` is logged. Once the main window shows, `WarnOfPendingMigrationsAsync` checks off the UI thread for migrations the database doesn't have yet, and says so: every screen reading a column it lacks would otherwise fail one error notice at a time.
- **A failure is shown on its screen in plain words, and the screen carries on** (2026-10-03, after a code review that found two ways the app failed badly). (1) `ProductsViewModel.LoadProductDetailAsync` and `RawMaterialsViewModel.LoadDetailsAsync` rethrew a failed read into `Observable.Switch(...).Subscribe()` with no error handler, which ended the subscription: from then on no pick loaded anything, `AreDetailsReady` stayed false, the detail tabs ignored the mouse and Save silently did nothing, until the app restarted. A probe that failed one read reproduced it on both screens; Sub-Assemblies' pick and Products' Existing Sub-Assembly and Change Link picks had the same shape. (2) No `LoadCommand` caught anything and nothing subscribed to `ThrownExceptions`, so a load that couldn't reach SQL Server ended in ReactiveUI's `UnhandledErrorException` on the dispatcher, which `App` only logged: the app closed. Now `ViewModelBase.LoadLatest` runs every selection-driven load (a failure shows and the next value loads as usual; a load a newer value cancelled is ignored, whatever it threw; a pick of nothing doesn't clear a failure's error, since Sub-Assemblies clears the pick when its read fails, and ReactiveUI delivers that nested change after the refresh guard has lifted), `LoadSafelyAsync` wraps every `LoadCommand` (a failure shows without clearing the notices, since a screen loads after its own Save; a later load that works clears it), and `RunSafelyAsync` catches everything. What's shown comes from `Failure.Of`: a `BusinessRuleException`'s message as written; SQL Server's errors by number (2601 and 2627 a duplicate, read as "That name is already in use." or "That abbreviation is already in use." by the index's name; 547 something in use, a reference gone, or a check refused; 515, 8115, 8152 and 2628, 1205, -2, 207 and 208, and the connection errors); SqlClient's refusal of a number too large for its column, which used to read as "That name is already in use."; a `TimeoutException`; and anything else, a bug, as "Something went wrong, and the details went to the log: …". A refusal is logged as a warning, anything else as an error with its stack. Reports and Print are `ViewModelBase`s now, with an `ErrorToast`. Tests: `LoadFailureTests` (on `FailingDbContextFactory`, which refuses contexts while it's set) and `FailureTests` (real database errors).
- **Confirmation dialogs stay in the View, via a shared extension method, not baked into each View's constructor by hand.** `ConfirmDeleteHandlerRegistration.RegisterNativeMessageBoxHandler(this ViewModelBase)` wires a `ViewModelBase`'s `ConfirmDelete` interaction to a native `MessageBox.Show(..., MessageBoxButton.YesNo, MessageBoxImage.Warning)` in one place. `RawMaterialsView`, `SubAssembliesView` and `ProductsView` each call `viewModel.RegisterNativeMessageBoxHandler();` once in their constructor, right after setting `DataContext`. The Settings dialog's tabs are the exception: `SettingsWindow` is built afresh each time it opens, over the same ViewModels, so it registers their handlers itself and disposes the registrations when it closes. That's why `RegisterNativeMessageBoxHandler` has returned its registration since the Settings dialog arrived (2026-09-13).
- **A start that fails says why and closes the app** (2026-11-03, at the user's request, from a code review). `App.OnStartup` runs everything up to the main window showing through `AppStartup.TryStart`: a failure is logged as fatal, `ShowError` shows its own first line and where the log is, and `Shutdown(1)` ends the process. Until then it reached `OnDispatcherUnhandledException`, which is for a running app: with `appsettings.json` missing, the app said "Couldn't write the file… close it in Excel… The app is still running" and stayed alive with no window until it was ended in Task Manager (reproduced, and checked fixed, by starting a copy without the file). `Failure.Of` no longer says every `IOException` is a file open elsewhere: only a sharing or lock violation does (`HResult` 0x80070020 or 0x80070021). A missing file or folder says so (`FileMissingText`), any other gives Windows' words, and a `PostgresToolException` gives the tool's. Tests: `AppStartupTests`, `FailureTests`.
- **One copy of the app at a time** (2026-11-04, at the user's request, from a code review). There are no concurrency tokens, so two copies side by side saved over each other with neither saying so. `App.OnStartup` claims the app first (`SingleInstanceGuard`, a named mutex, `Local\TinapayanRMS`), and a copy that can't asks the open one to come to the front and closes, before it has started or logged anything. Named kernel objects rather than a lock file, so a copy that crashes or is ended leaves nothing to clear up. **What a guard could break is why it's built as it is.** A restore restarts the app by starting the new copy from `OnExit`, while the old one is alive: `OnExit` lets go first. A copy opened while the first is still starting, which took 13 seconds cold in a Debug build, waits for its window, for up to 30 seconds. A frozen copy says so rather than the second copy quietly vanishing, so the open copy answers from its UI thread. If the open copy closes while being asked, the one asking takes over. A copy from another folder says where the open one runs from before bringing it forward, since the user's shortcut runs the published app, and `dotnet run` beside it would otherwise show the old build as if it were the new one. The window comes forward through Win32 (`IsIconic`, `ShowWindow`, `GetLastActivePopup`, `SetForegroundWindow`, with `AllowSetForegroundWindow` from the second copy), since running WPF's `Activate` and `SystemCommands.RestoreWindow` left the keyboard on a window a message box had disabled and left a minimized window with a dialog open minimized. **It's per Windows sign-in, the common case.** Another Windows user or another PC isn't stopped: only a check when saving could catch those, and the concurrency tokens that would need are still to do. Tests: `SingleInstanceGuardTests`, on real named objects, each copy on a thread of its own, the whole of what a copy does as it starts included (`Claim`). `App`'s wiring of it has no test, as `App` can't be started in one: it was checked in the real app across real processes (started twice, minimized, closed and reopened, killed, from another folder, frozen, restarted, and with a dialog or message box up), freezing and restarting through a `DOTNET_STARTUP_HOOKS` assembly so that no data was touched.
- **Export to Excel is answered by the View, as a confirmation is** (2026-10-03, at the user's request). A ViewModel only asks: `ViewModelBase.ExportToExcelAsync(title)` raises the `ExportToExcel` interaction and reports the path it gets back ("Saved … in …"), inside `RunSafelyAsync`, so a file open in Excel is an error notice (`IOException`, `Failure.FileInUseText`). The View answers (`Exporting/ExcelExportHandlerRegistration.RegisterExcelExport`) with a Save dialog and `Exporting/ExcelExport.Save`, because the grids are the View's: the sort, the checkbox filters and the hidden columns (Reports' Columns list) live on the `SfDataGrid`, not in the ViewModel. `Save` goes through Syncfusion's grid converter (`Syncfusion.DataGridExcelExport.WPF`, which brings XlsIO; the user's key validated for `Platform.Excel` before it was added) on the grid's `View`, so the sort and filters come with it, and does what the converter leaves undone, each found by decompiling `SfDataGridToExcelConverter` 35.1.37: hidden columns are written unless named in `ExcludeColumns`; a numeric column's format is `0.00_);(0.00)`, with no thousands separator, so it's set to `#,##0.00` from the column's `NumberDecimalDigits`; and the columns take the grid's on-screen widths, too narrow for Reports' wrapped headings on one line, so they're autofitted. `GridFillerRows`' padding is unbound rows, left out by `ExportUnBoundRows` false. Figures go in as numbers at full precision (the converter parses each value's text as a double). The workbook is saved to memory before the file is written, so a file open in Excel is left untouched. `SfTreeGrid` export isn't supported yet: its indent columns shift each level's cells, so the column formats would need finding by heading. `ExcelExportTests` checks a real grid read back with XlsIO.
- **A "replace-all" editor's referenced-entity validation must run *before* `ExecuteDeleteAsync`, never only implicitly via a dictionary lookup or a database FK constraint afterward** — a real, latent bug found and fixed in Phase 14's coverage audit. `PackagingLineRepository.SetLinesAsync` and `ProductSubAssemblyUsageRepository.SetUsagesForRoleAsync` (both Phase 8) already got this right: they build a lookup of the referenced entities that actually exist and loop over the incoming rows checking each one, throwing a friendly `InvalidOperationException` immediately, all *before* the old rows are deleted. `SubAssemblyLineRepository.SetLinesAsync` (Phase 7) and `IngredientBomLineRepository.SetLinesAsync` (Phase 6) did not: both deleted the existing lines first and only discovered a bad reference afterward — the former via a plain dictionary indexer throwing a raw, unfriendly `KeyNotFoundException`; the latter via the database's own `Restrict` FK constraint throwing `DbUpdateException` at `SaveChangesAsync`. In both cases the existing, valid rows were already gone by the time the rejection happened, with no transaction to roll them back. Neither path had a test covering "the new set references something that doesn't exist," which is exactly why it went unnoticed since Phase 6/7. Fixed by moving the existence-check dictionary/set build (and a `foreach` loop throwing `InvalidOperationException` per missing id) to before the `ExecuteDeleteAsync` call in both repositories, matching the established correct shape, with a regression test in each asserting the *existing* lines survive a rejected replacement.
- **A native `ProgressBar`, not a Syncfusion busy-indicator control, for Phase 13's busy-during-save indicator.** **Superseded (2026-09-14): no screen shows a busy indicator now.** The bar ended up in the status banner, and went with it, along with `ViewModelBase.IsBusy`, when the error toast replaced the banner: saves take well under a second, and buttons disable while they run (see the `ErrorToast` notes). The choice was deliberate while it lasted: the app references no Syncfusion busy-indicator package, and adding one for one small piece of UX polish wasn't worth it. A plain `ProgressBar IsIndeterminate="True" Height="4"` bound to `IsBusy` via `BooleanToVisibilityConverter`, placed in a status-bar row above each screen's content alongside the `ErrorMessage` `TextBlock`, is the sanctioned native-control fallback per the Conventions section below.
  - **Retrofitting a status-bar row onto an existing `Grid` needs `Grid.Row` added to every pre-existing child, not just the new row itself.** WPF defaults an unset `Grid.Row` to `0`; inserting a new `RowDefinition="Auto"` above the existing content and giving only the *new* status-bar element `Grid.Row="0"` leaves every already-existing child (which never had an explicit `Grid.Row` before, back when there was only one row) silently defaulting to `Grid.Row="0"` too, overlapping the status bar. Caught and fixed in `IngredientsView.xaml` by re-reading the file after the first edit; applied correctly from the start in `PackagingMaterialsView.xaml` and `SubAssembliesView.xaml` afterward. `ProductsView.xaml` doesn't use a column-based `Grid` at all (its panels are a horizontally-scrolling `ScrollViewer` of `StackPanel`s, per the CRUD screen pattern note above) — for that shape, wrap the existing `ScrollViewer` in an outer two-row `Grid` (status bar in row 0, the untouched `ScrollViewer` in row 1) instead of touching every inner panel.
  - **Sending synthetic `WM_LBUTTONDOWN`/`WM_LBUTTONUP` directly to a window's `HWND` (via `SendMessage`/`PostMessage`, with screen-to-client-translated coordinates) doesn't reliably drive a Syncfusion `ButtonAdv`'s click, even though it targets the exact window and never touches the real screen cursor** (tried in Phase 13, verifying the new validation/confirmation wiring while the user had a game occupying the foreground — see the "can't win against a real foreground app" lesson above). Syncfusion's WPF controls also don't expose the standard UI Automation `InvokePattern` (`GetSupportedPatterns` on a `ButtonAdv` returns only `SynchronizedInputPattern`), so `AutomationElement`-based invocation doesn't work either. Both were dead ends for exercising a write-path command without either real OS focus or a real physical click — which matches, and reinforces, the existing Phase 5/7 guidance to rely on integration tests plus a line-by-line binding review for write paths when the app can't safely be given real focus.

- **Vim keys: an opt-in key layer in the manner of Vimium** (2026-10-03, at the user's request: "Check the possibility of implementing Vim Mode to navigation and editing").
  - **What the user chose.** A navigation layer, not Vim editing inside fields. Esc in a cell or field keeps what was typed and returns to Normal. It's off until ticked in a Settings checkbox, kept per Windows user. Link hints (`f`), a command line (`:`) and a cheat sheet (`?`) are in; counts (`5j`) are not. Also asked for: "as responsive as possible".
  - **Where it lives.** It's all in `Vim/`.
    - `VimKeys` is registered from `App`'s static constructor.
    - `KeyboardSettingsViewModel` (Settings' **Keyboard** tab) keeps the flag in `IViewStateStore` under `vim-keys`, and `App.OnStartup` applies it.
    - While it's off, every handler returns after a single `bool` check. The app's behaviour, Esc included, is unchanged.
  - **Letters are read from a window-level `PreviewTextInput`.** `SfDataGrid` starts a cell edit for a letter or digit in `OnTextInput` (`char.IsLetterOrDigit`, then `CurrentCellManager.BeginEdit`; decompiled 35.1.37), so a tunnelling handler on the window sees the character first. `e.Text` is already the keyboard layout's character, so no `ToUnicodeEx` is needed. Named keys (Esc, Ctrl+R) come from `PreviewKeyDown`.
  - **Most keys are raised as the key they stand for.** `j` is Down; `dd` is Delete; in a tree, `o` is Enter and `r` is F2. Each is raised on the element that has the keyboard, as a `PreviewKeyDown` and then, unless handled, a `KeyDown`. That's what `KeyboardDevice.PostProcessInput` does with a real press (PresentationCore 10.0.8). So the grids, trees and the app's own behaviours (`GridFillerRows`, `GridUndoSteps`, `Tree_KeyDown`) react exactly as they do to the key.
  - **Only keys typed without a modifier are raised.** `KeyboardDevice.Modifiers` reads the physical keyboard. So Ctrl+Home can't be raised at all, and End raised for a `G` (Shift+g) arrives as Shift+End, which picks a range in the Extended trees.
    - `gg`, `G`, `0` and `$` use the grid's `MoveCurrentCell` with `GetFirstDataRowIndex`/`GetLastDataRowIndex` (the latter leaves out the add row) and the column helpers.
    - A tree picks its row through `SelectedItems`.
  - **`j`/`k` in a form call `MoveFocus` (Next/Previous), never arrows.** Syncfusion's number boxes step their value on Up/Down (`DoubleValueHandler.HandleKeyDown` → `HandleUpDownKey`).
  - **`SfTreeView` in Extended mode has three quirks** (its `SelectionController`, decompiled 35.1.37):
    - its Home moves only the focus border (`HandleCurrentItemFocusBorder`) and picks nothing;
    - its arrow keys do nothing while `CurrentItem` is hidden, for example by a search;
    - a `SelectedItem` set from outside is inserted in front of the rows already picked (`ProcessSelectionPropertiesChanged`).

    So `gg`, `G`, and `j`/`k` from a hidden row pick their row by clearing and refilling `SelectedItems`, as `SelectedTreeNode`'s setter does.
  - **Esc in a cell commits it** (`GridEditCommit.Commit(grid, keepCurrentCell: true)`), as clicking away does. Esc is handled only when it has something to do: drop a half-typed sequence, end a cell edit, or take a field to Normal. Otherwise a dialog's Cancel still gets it, so a prompt's text box needs two Escs.
  - **In a grid, Insert lasts from cell to cell until Esc** (2026-10-07, at the user's request: "when entering data in the Products Ingredient's line datagrid when vim's mode is insert, i still need to press i to begin inserting data"). Insert used to mean only "a cell is being edited", so Tab or Enter, which end the edit and move on, dropped the grid back to Normal. The next value's digits were then swallowed as Normal keys, and `i` was needed again. Now `_gridIsInsert` is set whenever a cell is seen being edited (`IsGridInsert`, however the edit began: `i`, a click under `EditTrigger="OnTap"`, a letter), and kept while the keyboard moves within the same grid (into a cell's editor and back). While it's set, letters and digits pass through, and `SfDataGrid.OnTextInput` starts the cell's edit as it does with the keys off. Esc clears it, ending the cell's edit if there is one, and is handled even when no cell is being edited, so a dialog's Cancel then needs a second Esc, as with a field. A grid reached from another place takes the mode the keys were in (`Arrive`), so Tab from a field in Insert goes on typing; turning the keys on starts it in Normal. Tests: `VimKeysGridTests.Insert_lasts_from_cell_to_cell_so_a_digit_on_the_next_cell_starts_typing_until_Esc`, `Esc_on_a_cell_not_being_typed_into_takes_Insert_back_to_Normal`.
  - **Ctrl+[ is Esc, and Ctrl+N and Ctrl+P are Down and Up in an open list, as in Vim** (2026-10-06, at the user's requests: "Vim has a keybinding <Ctrl>+[ for <Esc>, can we implement that?", then "Vim uses <Ctrl> + n for down and <Ctrl> + p for up" when the autocompletion popup is showing). They work only with the Vim keys on, like the rest of the layer. `VimKeys.KeyForChord` maps them. The list is open when `IsListOpen` says so: a `ComboBoxAdv`, `ComboBox`, `SfMultiColumnDropDownControl` or `SfTextBoxExt` suggestion list. Otherwise Ctrl+N and Ctrl+P are left alone. Ctrl+[ is `Key.OemOpenBrackets`, the key that types `[` on a US layout.
    - **`PressInstead` pushes the key's `PreviewKeyDown` through `InputManager.ProcessInput`**, which is re-entrant: it pushes a marker and processes up to it. Raising the event on the focused element (`RaiseKey`) isn't enough. `ButtonAdv.IsCancel` registers Esc with `AccessKeyManager` (`"\u001b"`), and that takes it only in the input manager's `PostProcessInput`.
    - **Ctrl is lifted from the thread's key state while the key goes through.** `KeyboardDevice.Modifiers` reads it live through `GetKeyState` (`Win32KeyboardDevice.GetKeyStatesFromSystem`, PresentationCore 10.0.8), which reads the thread's own table. So `GetKeyboardState`, the Ctrl bits cleared, `SetKeyboardState`, and the saved table put back after. Without that, Ctrl+N's Down arrived as Ctrl+Down, which jumps a list to its last row.
    - **A picker's key-ups after its highlight moves are swallowed until the next key-down** (`PickerMatchHighlight`). It used to swallow only the arrow's own key-up. Ctrl+N's key-ups are N and Ctrl, and `ComboBoxAdv.OnKeyUp` refilters by any text that differs from `oldFilter`, which only its own `OnPreviewKeyDown` brings up to date. So the list shrank to the one name the move wrote into the box. A real Ctrl+Down or Shift+Down did the same when the modifier came up.
    - Verified in the app on the secondary monitor: Ctrl+[ closed the cheat sheet, the hints, the command bar and Settings, and took a field from Insert to Normal keeping its text. In Raw Materials' Category, "c" then Ctrl+N, Ctrl+N and Ctrl+P walked the list as Down, Down and Up do, every row still listed. In Purchase Cost Setup's Purchase Unit cell, opened with F4, Ctrl+N, Ctrl+N and Ctrl+P went gram, bag, gram, and a real Down then went to bag.
  - **Link hints click through `ButtonBase.OnClick`, by reflection.** `ButtonAdvAutomationPeer` provides no Invoke pattern. A `SplitButtonAdv` is clicked through its own protected `OnClick` too, which leaves `IsPressed` set, so it's cleared through `IsPressedProperty` because the setter isn't public. A `DropDownButtonAdv` gets `IsDropDownOpen`.
  - **A hint on a picker puts the keyboard in its `PART_Editable` box, not on the picker** (`VimKeys.FocusPicker`; 2026-10-07, found by the user: Products' Category hint did nothing). An editable `ComboBoxAdv` hands focus on to its box only while it's focusable, and a Shift+Tab out of the box makes it unfocusable for good: `Part_IsEdit_PreviewKeyDown` sets `Focusable = false` so the Shift+Tab doesn't land back on the picker, and only a change to `IsEditable`, `AllowMultiSelect` or `EnableToken` sets it back. `picker.Focus()` was then refused. Typing, clicking and Tab still got in, through the box. Found in an off-screen probe running `ComboBoxAdv`'s own handler with Shift held; pinned by `VimKeysPickerTests`.
  - **The command line** runs the screen's own commands through `ICommand.Execute`, as their buttons do. The screen is found as Ctrl+Z finds its own: `IRecordScreen` (New, Save, Delete, `OpenByName` via `RecordNameMatch`) and `IRevertScreen`. The windows take part through `IVimHost`. Enter always closes the line. What went wrong (a parse error, or why the command couldn't run, such as `:w` with nothing to save) then shows in the bar in its place, without the box, until the next key, a click or 5 seconds. This changed on 2026-10-16 at the user's request: the line used to stay open with the message beside the command.
  - **Ctrl+R = is Vim's expression register** (2026-10-22, at the user's request: "copy Vim's expression register to insert math results … press Enter to insert the result directly", after first asking for an `=5*(2+3)` formula in a grid cell). In Insert, Ctrl+R waits for a register (`"` in the indicator) and `=` opens the bar with a `=` prompt; Enter types the answer at the caret, over the selection, as typing would. A formula typed straight into a number cell was rejected: `DoubleTextBox` masks every key and reformats its own text, so `=`, `(` and `*` never reach it. **The bar captures keys like the hints, and the keyboard stays in the cell**: focus moved to a box in the bar would end the edit through `GridEditCommit` and lose the caret. **Division is decimal** (`7/2` is 3.5), not Vim's whole-number division, since costs and grams need decimals. The answer is typed through `TextCompositionManager` so the box's own mask, limits and undo apply, rounded half away from zero to the decimals the box takes (`MaximumNumberDecimalDigits`, else `NumberDecimalDigits`), or the mask would cut it short. A sum that can't be worked out keeps the bar open with the reason. See the developer manual's "Vim keys".
  - **Responsiveness.**
    - Where the keyboard is gets worked out once per focus change.
    - A key is a `FrozenDictionary` lookup and its action, done within the key's own event.
    - The hints are drawn by one element in `OnRender`, and the overlays are built once per window.
    - Debug builds log any key taking over 4 ms. In the app, most took 4–10 ms, and the rest was the screen's own work (a row picked loads its form). Thirty `j` presses down the Raw Materials list landed on the 30th row in under a second, none dropped.
  - **UI Automation's `FocusedElement` reports the window while an `SfDataGrid` has the keyboard**, so a script can't use it to tell where the keyboard is in a grid.
  - **Verified in the app** on the secondary monitor, against the dev database, with nothing saved:
    - turning the keys on and off in Settings, and the setting applied at startup;
    - `gT`, `j`/`k`, `i`, Esc and `?` in Settings, and Esc closing it once nothing was being typed;
    - hints on Raw Materials and Products, including a list row, a grid row, a drop-down button and the split button's label;
    - `j`/`k`/`h`/`l`/`gg`/`G`/`o` in the lists, including after a search hid the current row;
    - `$`/`0`/`j`/`k`/`i`/Esc in Purchase Cost Setup, with Save and Undo still off after an Esc that changed nothing;
    - `/`, `:e sugar`, `:w` with nothing to save, an unknown command, `:pro`, `:raw`, `:set`, `gt`, `H` and `L`.
  - **Tests:**
    - pure: `VimKeySequenceTests`, `VimKeymapTests`, `VimCommandLineTests`, `HintLabelsTests`, `RecordNameMatchTests`, `KeyboardSettingsViewModelTests`;
    - `VimKeysGridTests`, in a real off-screen grid, and `VimKeysPickerTests`, in a real off-screen picker, both in `OffscreenGridCollection`;
    - an `OpenByName` test in each screen's refresh tests.

## Reactive Costing Cascade

The core design problem is that a price change on any ingredient or packaging material must ripple up through everything that uses it, automatically. The cascade has two starting points, one per raw material type, and both end at the product:

```
Price entry added for an ingredient
  → purchased ingredient's CostPerBaseUnit (per gram) recomputed
    → every made ingredient that uses it recomputed, up every level of the BOM tree (its sub-assembly's lines and its add-ins)
  ─ stored above this line, worked out on read below it ─
      → every sub-assembly using a changed ingredient, from its lines
        → every ProductSubAssemblyUsage of that sub-assembly ─────┐
                                                                  ├→ Product total cost
Price entry added for a packaging material                        │    → Product margin / selling price
  → packaging material's CostPerBaseUnit recomputed (stored)      │
    → every PackagingLine using it ───────────────────────────────┘
```

Only raw materials' costs are stored: a purchased one's from its prices, and a made or linked one's by the cascade. Everything above them, a sub-assembly's batch weight and cost included since 2026-11-05, is worked out from its lines whenever it's read, so the one propagator ends at the ingredients.

**Quantity Required hangs off the end of that cascade** (2026-09-20), and adds nothing to it. It is a plain multiplier applied where the branches meet — `CalculateMaterialCost` multiplies the usages' cost per piece by it and adds the packaging once — so a product that sells in boxes of 8 moves whenever its piece does, with no propagator of its own. Nothing about it is stored derived, and no write path has to know it exists.

This replaced packs (2026-09-18 to 2026-09-20), which hung off the same end: a pack was priced from the product's material cost per piece by `PackMaterialCost = materialCostPerPiece × PiecesPerPack + that pack's own line costs`. The one cost that was a pack's alone was its own packaging lines (`PackagingLine.PackId` set), which rode the packaging branch exactly as the per-piece lines did, and `PackagingLineRepository.GetEffectiveForProductAsync` returned `PackId is null` lines only to keep the per-piece figure honest. With packs gone every packaging line is the product's, and that filter went with them.

The original idea was to model this with DynamicData `SourceCache<T>`s joined via `.Connect()`/`.InnerJoin()`/`.TransformMany()`, so derived observable collections update themselves. **That's not what got built.** Phase 6 established a different, simpler pattern first — a pure ordering function plus a stateful database orchestrator, described below — and it worked well enough that Phase 11 extended the *same* shape to the sub-assembly branch instead of introducing DynamicData joins partway through (that branch went on 2026-11-05, below). EF Core stays the single source of truth throughout; nothing here lives only in an in-memory observable.

**Two costs go up the tree side by side** (since the buffered cost, 2026-09-14): the true `CostPerBaseUnit` and the `BufferedCostPerBaseUnit`. A purchased material's buffered cost is a fixed amount since 2026-09-19 (see Buffered cost under RawMaterial): `RawMaterialRules.ApplyBrandPrices` sets it only on a first price, and the Raw Materials Save writes it otherwise. Every write through the cascade sets both: `IngredientCostPropagator`, which runs `IngredientBomRules.CalculateCost` twice per made ingredient (true costs, then buffered costs — with nothing of its own on top since 2026-09-21, and its stored `CostBufferPercent` normalised to 0 in the same pass). Everything downstream reads the buffered one: a sub-assembly's batch cost and line costs, and packaging line costs, in Products and Reports. The one exception is on read only: the Products screen's **Unbuffered** switch (2026-09-29) prices what it shows and prints from the true `CostPerBaseUnit` instead. Nothing stored changes, and the propagator doesn't know it exists. A buffer edit is one more way a cost changes: `RawMaterialRepository.UpdateAsync` reprices a purchased material's buffered cost itself and asks for the cascade for any ingredient whose buffer changed (packaging needs none, as below).

The one nesting exception is made ingredients: their BOMs nest to any depth, so that part of the cascade can't be a fixed one-hop join. An ingredient's cost can change from a new price entry, a BOM line edit, a `WeightLossPercent` edit, or a linked ingredient's link or share edit; when it does, the made ingredients that depend on it need recomputing in dependency order, components before the ingredients built from them. Sub-assemblies don't nest (a `SubAssemblyLine` only ever references an `Ingredient`, never another `SubAssembly` — Phase 7), so they needed no ordering logic at all: the cascade recomputed every sub-assembly touched until 2026-11-05, and nothing has needed to since.

**Made ingredients made from sub-assemblies needed no new ordering either** (2026-10-27). A made ingredient's BOM is read as its sub-assembly's lines plus its add-ins (`IngredientBoms`), expanded to ingredient edges, so a sub-assembly is never a node: `IngredientCostGraph`, `IngredientCostRules.Recompute`/`Reprice` and `IngredientBomRules.CalculateCost` are unchanged, and the "nesting sub-assemblies needs one mixed ordering" objection recorded under Move to In-House doesn't arise. A made ingredient is costed from its lines' ingredients' costs, never from a sub-assembly's batch cost, which is buffered only and would break the dependency order. **The one new trigger** is a sub-assembly's lines changing, which now changes the BOM of every made ingredient made from it: `SubAssemblyLineRepository.SetLinesAsync`, which every line write goes through (the Sub-Assemblies Save, a product's Save relining a linked one, role adoption), checks each such made ingredient for a loop through the new lines (`IngredientBomRules.EnsureNotInOwnRecipe`, by name, then `IngredientDependencies.EnsureNoCycleAsync`) and cascades it (`IngredientCostPropagator.RecomputeAndPropagateAsync`) on the caller's context, before its transaction commits. A made ingredient's own Save checks the same as a whole after every part is written (`RawMaterialRepository.EnsureMadeIngredientFitsAsync`). A sub-assembly a made ingredient is made from can't be deleted (`SubAssemblyRules.EnsureNoMadeIngredientMadeFrom`) or adopted by a product when its role turns product-owned (`SubAssemblyRepository.SingleUse`). Covered by `MadeIngredientLinkPersistenceTests` (which replaced `IngredientBomLinePersistenceTests`), three `ReactiveCostCascadeTests` and `CostCascadeTransactionPersistenceTests.A_sub_assembly_line_edit_whose_made_ingredients_cascade_fails_is_not_saved_either`.

**Ingredient branch — implemented Phase 6** as `IngredientCostGraph.GetDependentsInOrder` (`ApplicationCore`, pure graph logic, no I/O — reverse-BFS from the changed ingredient to find every made ingredient that transitively depends on it, then a DFS-based topological sort restricted to that set) and `IngredientCostPropagator.RecomputeAndPropagateAsync` (`Infrastructure`, the orchestrator, given one changed ingredient or the set one save changed (Re-apply Target Buffers, a sub-assembly several made ingredients are made from): loads every ingredient and every BOM line once, calls the graph function for each changed one, walks one dependency order over everything they reach (each ingredient recomputed once, however many changed) recomputing each ingredient's cost per gram (`CostPerBaseUnit`) from its own lines — reading each component's cost directly off the already-loaded, already-correct dictionary, never re-walking that component's own tree — then a single `SaveChangesAsync`). Every write path that can change an ingredient's cost calls it: `RawMaterialPriceHistoryRepository`'s add/update/delete (for an ingredient's price), `RawMaterialRepository.UpdateAsync` when `Source == Made` (a `WeightLossPercent` edit), `RawMaterialUnitRepository.UpdateAsync` when an ingredient unit's new multiplier reprices it (its latest price is in that unit), and `IngredientBomLineRepository.SetLinesAsync`. (Before the raw materials merge these were `IngredientPriceHistoryRepository` and `IngredientRepository`.) It loads ingredients only (`Type == Ingredient`), since packaging never takes part.

**Linked ingredients needed no new ordering** (2026-09-16). A linked ingredient's link is one more edge in the same graph: `IngredientCostPropagator` adds `linked → linked-to` beside every `made → component` before calling `IngredientCostGraph.GetDependentsInOrder`, which is unchanged, so egg white is recomputed after the egg and a made ingredient using egg white after egg white. In the walk, a linked ingredient is costed with `RawMaterialRules.LinkedCost` from its linked-to ingredient's already-fresh true cost, takes that ingredient's buffer percent, and its buffered cost is its cost with that percent on top, and a made one with the BOM rollup as before. A linked ingredient that's itself the changed one is recomputed first, as a made one is: `RawMaterialRepository` asks for the cascade when a linked ingredient is added or updated.

**Sub-assembly branch — implemented Phase 11, removed 2026-11-05** (at the user's request, from an independent review of the code). `SubAssemblyCostPropagator` kept each sub-assembly's stored `TotalWeight`/`TotalCost` current: `IngredientCostPropagator` called it at the end of every run with the ingredients it had changed, `SubAssemblyLineRepository.SetLinesAsync` set them with each line save, and Recalculate Costs recomputed them all. Product costing never read them: it prices every section from its lines (`SectionFigures.Of`, through `SubAssemblyRules.CalculateTotals`), at the buffered or the true cost as the screen asks, which a buffered-only stored total couldn't serve. Their only readers were the Recipe API's mix batch weight, `SubAssemblyRepository.GetLinkableAsync`'s `total_weight > 0` filter and two list-row properties nothing bound, so the columns went (migration `DropStoredSubAssemblyTotals`) with the propagator: the API sums a mix's lines, and the filter asks for a line (`EXISTS`, the same thing, since a line's grams are positive by a check constraint). Every stored total in the dev data matched its lines the day they went.

**Product and packaging branches needed no propagation code at all.** Neither `ProductSubAssemblyUsage`'s cost-per-piece, `PackagingLine`'s cost-per-piece, a product's total material cost, nor its wastage (the shop-wide `CostingDefaults.WastagePercent` of its sub-assemblies, since 2026-10-23, read with every product) is stored anywhere (Phase 8's "derived values are computed on read" decision) — they're plain `ProductRules` functions reading the ingredients' costs (through each section's lines) and the packaging material's `CostPerBaseUnit` fresh every time a screen asks. As long as the raw materials' costs are correct — which the ingredient propagator guarantees, and which the price history repository has guaranteed for packaging since Phase 5 (`RawMaterialPriceHistoryRepository` since the raw materials merge, which skips the ingredient cascade for a packaging price) — a product's displayed cost is automatically correct too, with nothing to propagate into. Verified end-to-end (`ReactiveCostCascadeTests.cs`): a price change several BOM levels deep reaches a sub-assembly's cost with no direct edit to that sub-assembly, and a product's live-computed cost-per-piece reflects it immediately, with zero product-level or packaging-level code involved. Since 2026-09-14 a usage's weight per piece is itself computed on read (`ProductRules.ResolvePieceSize`, from the typed figure and the current batch weight), so a product that typed a yield sees its piece weight, and so its cost per piece, move when the owner changes the batch — with nothing to propagate either. Sub-assembly ownership didn't change the cascade at all: a linked product reads the same lines.

**Composite products needed no propagation either** (2026-09-29). A composite stores only its lines (a product and a quantity). What each line brings is its product's recipe cost, and its packaging cost when the composite includes its products' packaging, worked out on read through `SavedProductReader` like the product's own figures, so a price change reaches a composite the moment it reaches the products it holds, however deep. The Products screen reads the lines' costs again with every `LoadCommand`.

**Add-ins needed no propagation either, for the same reason** (2026-09-16). A `UsageAddInLine` stores only `UsageId`, `IngredientId` and `Grams`; its cost is `Grams × the ingredient's BufferedCostPerBaseUnit`, read fresh, and no derived total about it is stored anywhere. So the propagator is untouched, and a sub-assembly's own batch is its `SubAssemblyLines` alone — an add-in must never move the batch of a sub-assembly another product owns; only the linking usage's effective batch takes it (`ProductRules.CalculateEffectiveBatch`). `ReactiveCostCascadeTests` pins both halves: repricing an add-in's ingredient moves the linking usage's cost per piece, and leaves the sub-assembly's own batch cost exactly where it was.

**The cascade runs in the transaction of the save that causes it** (2026-10-03, after a code review). The propagators (two until 2026-11-05) were DI singletons that each opened a context of their own, so every write path committed first and cascaded after: a cascade that failed part-way (a dropped connection, a timeout, the app closed mid-way) left the save in and the stored costs above it stale, and nothing put them right, since saving again unchanged doesn't cascade. They became internal static classes that take the caller's context, and every caller (`RawMaterialRepository`'s add, update and Save, `RawMaterialBrandPriceRepository.SetAsync`, `RawMaterialUnitRepository.UpdateAsync`, `IngredientBomLineRepository.SetLinesAsync`) runs them before `CommitAsync`: a save and its cascade commit together or not at all (`CostCascadeTransactionPersistenceTests`, which fail a cascade part-way with a save interceptor). A stored cost is set at the column's scale (`RawMaterialRules.StoredCost`), so a figure worked out again from unchanged inputs isn't written or dated again. The dev database had nothing out of step the same day (70 sub-assemblies, 5 made ingredients, checked by SQL).

**Recalculate Costs is the safety net for a change made outside the app** (2026-10-03): Settings → Costing Defaults → **Recalculate Costs** (`ICostRecalculator`, `CostRecalculator`) recomputes every made and linked ingredient in dependency order (`IngredientCostGraph.InDependencyOrder`, the cascade's own ordering for a whole set), in one transaction, and says how many were wrong (every sub-assembly's stored totals after them, until those went on 2026-11-05). Purchased materials aren't touched: their cost comes straight from their own saved prices. Settings is a modal dialog, so no save on another screen can run while it does. Its card, **Stored Costs**, sits to the right of the Costing Defaults card, each 420px wide and 16px apart in a `WrapPanel` (2026-10-06, at the user's request; it was under it). Settings opens 1000 wide, room for both; narrowed toward its 760 minimum, Stored Costs wraps under the defaults, and the tab scrolls (a `ScrollViewer`, which the stacked cards lacked: Recalculate Costs was cut off at the window's default size).

**The Price Simulator runs the cascade's math without storing it** (2026-10-03). A made or linked ingredient's costs are worked out by `IngredientCostRules.Recompute` (ApplicationCore), which `IngredientCostPropagator` stores and `IngredientCostRules.Reprice` applies to copies (`RawMaterial.ShallowCopy`) of the materials a simulated change reaches, in `IngredientCostGraph` order. One piece of code for both, so a simulation can't price an ingredient differently from how a real change would cascade. Its report (2026-10-06) names the changes reaching each product with `IngredientCostRules.ReachedBy`, the same dependency graph `Reprice` walks. No stored sub-assembly figure is involved: product costing prices every section from its lines' per-gram costs.

This does mean a product **already on screen** only reflects a price change made from a *different* screen once the user reselects it (or navigates back to it) — there's no cross-screen live push. That's the one piece of the original DynamicData-joins vision this phase deliberately didn't build; revisit only if that specific UX gap is ever reported as a real problem, not preemptively.

## Development Environment

- Development happens directly on a local Windows machine that has the .NET 11 SDK (11.0.100-rc.3.26522.148, a preview) and PostgreSQL 18 (EDB's installer, service `postgresql-x64-18`, `localhost:5432`) installed. The app, `dotnet ef` and the tests connect as the `tinapayanrms` role (`CREATEDB`, not a superuser), whose password is in `%APPDATA%\postgresql\pgpass.conf`, so no connection string carries one. `psql`, `pg_dump` and `pg_restore` are in `C:\Program Files\PostgreSQL\18\bin`, which isn't on the PATH. The same server holds the sibling projects' databases (`tinapayanrms_web*`, `tinapayanhub*`).
  - **Moved from SQL Server Express to PostgreSQL** (2026-10-21, at the user's request: "Let's drop the SQL Server as the database and use the Postgresql"). The patterns came from the sibling `TinapayanRMS_Web` project, which already ran this domain on PostgreSQL:
    - **Naming:** snake_case throughout (`UseSnakeCaseNamingConvention`, at the user's request: "can we use the snake case convention of postgresql?"). `TinapayanRMSDbContext.Configure` is the one place the provider is set up, for the app, `dotnet ef` and the tests. It also sets `SetPostgresVersion(18, 0)` (2026-10-28), since Npgsql 11 otherwise writes its SQL and migrations for PostgreSQL 16.
    - **Collation and timestamps:** every text column has the nondeterministic ICU collation `case_insensitive` (`und-u-ks-level2`), so the unique name indexes and the name sort stay case-insensitive and accent-sensitive, as SQL Server's `SQL_Latin1_General_CP1_CI_AS` was. Every `DateTime` is `timestamp without time zone`, since the app stamps local time (`TinapayanRMSDbContext.LocalNow()`, from its `Clock`, the system's unless a test passes a `TestClock`, since 2026-10-28).
    - **Migrations:** the 60-odd SQL Server migrations (with `nvarchar` types and hand-written T-SQL) were deleted and replaced by one generated `InitialCreate`.
    - **Dapper SQL:** rewritten in snake_case. Id lists became an `int[]` with `unnest` instead of `STRING_SPLIT`. The recursive CTEs gained `WITH RECURSIVE`, and `RawMaterialRepository`'s `feeds` follows BOM and link edges through one union, since PostgreSQL lets a recursive query name itself only once.
    - **Npgsql 11** (2026-10-26, from its release notes): a deadlock (40P01) or serialization failure (40001) raised during `SaveChanges`, even inside the app's own transaction, now comes as a `DbUpdateConcurrencyException` with the `PostgresException` inside it. `Failure` reads that inner code before its "changed or deleted on another screen" case, so a clash between two serializable `ProductVersionRepository` saves still says the database was busy. The execution strategy no longer wraps a transient failure in an `InvalidOperationException`, so a server lost mid-query reads as unreachable rather than as a bug. `Configure` sets `SetPostgresVersion(18, 0)`, since the provider otherwise assumes 16. The release's other features (JSON indexes and functions on `ToJson` types, temporal constraints, the new LINQ translations) were weighed and left alone: the version snapshot is only ever read whole, price history holds dates rather than periods, and the reads are Dapper SQL.
    - **Errors and backups:** `Failure` maps SQLSTATE codes. Backups are `pg_dump`/`pg_restore` (see the backups notes below).
    - **The data:** the dev data was copied over by a one-off file-based app: every table, ids included, by binary `COPY` in one transaction as `postgres` with `session_replication_role = replica`, and identity sequences set past the highest id. Each table's rows were then hashed on both sides and matched. The only loss is the times: SQL Server's 100 ns ticks became PostgreSQL's microseconds (242 values, all sub-microsecond). The stale `tinapayanrms` and `tinapayanrms_test` databases left from the 2026-09-27 attempt were dropped first.
    - **SQL Server Express** (with the dev data as it was on 2026-10-21) **and LocalDB are still installed, unused.**
  - **Why SQL Server Express, not LocalDB** (superseded by PostgreSQL on 2026-10-21) (2026-09-28, at the user's request). The app used LocalDB (`(localdb)\mssqllocaldb`) until then. The dev database was moved with a `COPY_ONLY` backup restored onto `.\SQLEXPRESS`, which kept its `SQL_Latin1_General_CP1_CI_AS` collation (the instance's own default is `Latin1_General_CI_AS`, so the test database, created by the fixture's `MigrateAsync`, gets that one; both are case-insensitive and accent-sensitive). Every table was checked identical afterwards. `AUTO_CLOSE` was turned off on `TinapayanRMS` and `TinapayanRMS_Test`: Express turns it on for its databases, and on a service instance it shuts an idle database down and empties its cache, so the next read after a pause is slow. It replaced an unrelated `TinapayanRMS` prototype database from before this repo (created 2026-08-31, with a `Recipe`/`RecipeVersion` schema), which the user chose to drop. LocalDB is still installed and holds its copy, unused.
  - A day earlier (2026-09-27) the app was briefly moved to PostgreSQL. That was reverted before it was committed (2026-09-28, at the user's request) and is kept only as a git stash.
- Building a runnable app requires the `SYNCFUSION_LICENSE_KEY` user environment variable to be set, since the build compiles the key in (see Tech Stack). The solution builds without it, but that app's `App` static constructor throws on startup, and `dotnet publish` fails outright.
- **Docker is not used anywhere in the project**: not for development, testing, or deployment.
- **Debug builds write WPF's binding errors to the log** (`BindingErrorLog`, added in `App`'s static constructor, 2026-09-25). WPF traces them only while a debugger is attached, and the app is mostly run without one. They land in `src/TinapayanRMS.UI/bin/Debug/<tfm>/win-x64/logs/tinapayanrms-<date>.log` as `WPF binding Error …` warnings.
  - **The first walk through every screen (2026-09-25, off-screen, against the dev database) found one real kind, now fixed.** Five `DoubleTextBox`es bound to a `decimal?` (Raw Materials' Weight Loss %, Weight Share %, Cost Share %, Cost Buffer % and Buffered Cost) threw in WPF's default decimal-to-double conversion each time the value was null: an `InvalidCastException` caught per update. Their bindings now set `TargetNullValue={x:Null}`, which hands a null straight to the box without converting. Any new `DoubleTextBox` over a `decimal?` needs the same.
  - **Expected at every startup, and harmless**: one `Cannot find governing FrameworkElement` or path error per `GridMultiColumnDropDownList.ItemsSource` or `GridCheckBoxColumn.AllowEditing` binding while its grid is built (a column isn't in the element tree until Syncfusion gives it the grid's `DataContext`; every column ended up with the right list or flag), and `TabItemExt`'s own `FindAncestor` warning from Syncfusion's template.
- **Off-screen harnesses** are file-based apps (`dotnet run Probe.cs`, referencing the UI project with `#:project`) that build `App` and `CompositionRoot` and show windows off-screen without activating them. The 2026-09-19 measurements and the 2026-09-25 leak and binding checks used them.
  - **`new App()` posts WPF's startup to the dispatcher**, so the first `Dispatcher.Run()` also runs `App.OnStartup`, which builds its own host and shows the real main window on screen (found 2026-09-25). Collect what `new App()` posts with `Dispatcher.CurrentDispatcher.Hooks.OperationPosted` and `Abort()` it before running the dispatcher.
  - Stand in for the main window with an off-screen `Window` themed like it (`SfSkinManager.SetTheme`). Otherwise the theme's shared brushes keep a strong reference to the first window that uses them, which reads as a leak.
  - `dotnet run` of a file-based app doesn't notice changes in a referenced project: pass `--no-cache` after editing the app.
  - Don't `ShowDialog` in a harness: it activates the window and takes focus from whatever the user is doing.
  - **Three more things a harness needs** (found 2026-10-24, checking every two-way number box on the real views for the .NET 11 decimal change). `#:property PublishAot=false`: a file-based app is published with Native AOT by default, and WPF can't be trimmed (NETSDK1168). A `DispatcherSynchronizationContext` on its STA thread before anything loads: without one a ViewModel's `await` comes back on the thread pool, and its collections throw ("a different thread owns it"), which `LoadSafelyAsync` turns into an empty screen. And the UI assembly as `Application.ResourceAssembly` (through its `_resourceAssembly` field, since WPF has already set it to the harness): a view's `/Assets/...` image is looked up there.
- EF Core migrations are generated with `dotnet ef migrations add`, not written by hand, and applied to the local database with `dotnet ef database update`. Run both from the repo root with `--project src/TinapayanRMS.Infrastructure`; that project's design-time `TinapayanRMSDbContextFactory` supplies the DbContext.
- **A phase is done** when the solution builds without errors, all tests pass, and any new migration applies cleanly to a local database. Mark it Done in the Build Plan table; there are no separate per-phase documents.
- **In-app backups go to `%ProgramData%\TinapayanRMS\Backups`** (2026-10-03). **Since 2026-10-21 they are `pg_dump` custom-format `.backup` files** (`DatabaseBackups` and `PostgresTools`, from `TinapayanRMS_Web`). `pg_dump` writes them as the user running the app, so `BackupFolder` now only gives `BUILTIN\Users` Modify. A restore goes into a `<db>_restoring` scratch database, is brought up to date there, and is swapped in by renaming, so a refused or failed restore never touches the data. The SQL Server `.bak` files aren't listed or restorable. The rest of this paragraph describes the SQL Server arrangement: the manual `COPY_ONLY` backups recorded above went to the instance's own Backup folder because the SQL service account (`NT SERVICE\MSSQL$SQLEXPRESS`) can't write to the user's profile; the app's folder works because the app creates it and grants that account Modify itself (`BackupFolder`), which needs no administrator, since a folder's owner may always change its permissions.
